The **pipeline's scope is stated**: Queue and Shipped are project-wide directory counts belonging to no session, while the phase comes from `state.md`, one repo-global file that names its owning session — and the note now names it, so a phase is not silently attached to whichever lanes sit below. And a spawned agent with no events yet reads **`spawned, no events recorded yet`** rather than `0 events · last unknown`: that is a state, not a gap.

**Deliberately NOT in this release: showing more than one session at a time.** The measurement that motivates it is recorded here so it is not re-derived — 3 sessions active within 4 hours, 14 within a day, 1 shown. Per-session *phase* is the blocker and it is a data problem, not a rendering one: `phase_transition` is agent-written and this repo's logs carry **6 of them across 183 sessions, none attributable to a session**, against 11,676 hook-written `token_ledger` lines. Deriving phase from events would be a guess — a Phase 4.5 heal worker emits the same `subtask_complete` as a Phase 3 worker, so Execute and Review are indistinguishable in the stream. Multi-session therefore waits on per-run state being recorded, not on the page.

**Tests.** `test-build-floor.sh` (za) proves the older plugin run is shown over the newer chat, with `selection` and the set-aside count; (zb) proves the fail-open path; **(zc) removes the preference and requires the newer chat back**, so (za) measures the rule rather than a coincidence of the fixture. `test-setup-ui.sh` (j51) pins all four page claims by literal and **(j52)** restores the label-repeating cell and requires detection — mutated on an ASCII anchor, because a pattern carrying the multibyte marker can silently match nothing and leave the control reporting "inconclusive". Suites: build-floor 358 → 366, setup-ui 465 → 469 (the first draft of this entry claimed a 462 baseline, carried over from an older run — measured on `main` it is 465). Verified in a browser against real projector output for both the coinciding (`Execute ●`) and differing (`Review FINALIZE`) phase cases.

**v15.56.1 — the identity guard compared the line, not the identity:** `emit-agent-identity.sh` shipped in v15.56.0 with a dedupe that matched the whole emitted line byte-for-byte, which the line's own `recorded_at` defeats: two firings a second apart describe the SAME agent with two different timestamps, so both were written. **CI caught it and the local run did not** — three back-to-back firings all landed inside one wall-clock second on macOS, so the guard matched and the case passed; on Linux they straddled a second and the case failed `expected 1, actual 2`. The identity of the row is `(cc_session_id, agent_id, agent_type)`; the timestamp is metadata about when it was noticed, and it is now excluded from the comparison. The scan parses each line rather than grepping a substring, so field order and any future additive field cannot silently break the match, and `fromjson? // empty` skips a malformed neighbour instead of aborting; every failure path still falls through to the append, because a duplicate identity row is harmless (the projector takes the FIRST type it sees) and a missing one is not. **The test now forces the straddle with an explicit `sleep 1` between firings, and asserts that the `recorded_at` values really did differ** — without that second assertion the case would silently become the old, clock-dependent one again. `test-agent-identity.sh` 22 -> 23.

**v15.56.0 — the lane identity was on a hook nobody registered:** The Floor rendered `identity unknown` on almost every lane, and the standing explanation — the `SubagentStop` payload carries no `agent_type` — was **true and led to the wrong conclusion**. Measured across one real project's whole history: 1,040 distinct agent ids, **64** carrying a type and **976** not. Two candidate repairs were tested and both fail. The `agent-<id>.meta.json` sidecar Claude Code writes beside a subagent transcript does carry `agentType`, but it intersects the typed set **64/64** and the untyped set **0/976** — an agent has a sidecar exactly when the answer is already known, so the lookup re-derives what is known and resolves nothing. And nothing else on disk records what those agents were, so no back-fill of existing lanes is possible in any form.

**The identity is on a third hook the plugin never registered.** A single `PostToolUse[Task]` payload carries **both halves at once** — `tool_input.subagent_type` (the role) and `tool_response.agentId`, an id in the SAME namespace as `SubagentStop.agent_id`. No join across events, no correlation store, no third hook. The evidence was already committed: `scripts/progress-event-fixtures/spawn-probe-2026-09-02/` holds six real captures from one run, and `docs/SPIKES/AGENT_SPAWN_PAYLOAD_PROBE.md` records the measurement. **That spike closed NO-GO correctly — against a different requirement** (a live spawn-time `agent_spawn` event joinable at spawn time), which is why this reading of the same evidence was never taken. Every positive test below is driven by those captures rather than a hand-written payload, because hand-reasoning about this payload has now been wrong three times.

**`emit-agent-identity.sh` (hooks 26 → 27) writes ONE line and the projector joins it at read time.** It writes a line rather than enriching the stop line because `PostToolUse[Task]` and `SubagentStop` both fire when a subagent finishes **in an unguaranteed order** — an enriching emitter would race. `build-floor.sh` already took `agent_type` from ANY line of an agent, so the join needed **no new read path**; what it needed was an exclusion, because such a line is a fact ABOUT an agent rather than an event OF one and a count just corrected for a 3x inflation must not gain a different +1. The line carries **no `ts`**, which keeps it out of `first_ts`/`last_ts` and out of the newest-session selection by construction rather than by a second filter; the time is kept under `recorded_at`. One projector change was genuinely required and was found by running the test rather than by reading the code: the line classifier projected only `{sid, ts, agent_id, agent_type, agent_scope, branch}` and **dropped `event`**, so the exclusion could never fire.

**The trap the spike had already caught, honoured here: the matcher is `Task` and the payload reports `tool_name: "Agent"`.** A consumer filtering on that field never fires, so this emitter does not read it — and the fixture premise asserts the value is still `Agent`, so the day it changes the reason is not lost. Both halves are required (a type with no id cannot be attached to a lane; an id with no type says nothing new), a hostile id is refused rather than sanitised, and a retried tool call is idempotent. New suite `test-agent-identity.sh` (22 assertions, every positive one fixture-driven) plus `test-build-floor.sh` case (z) for the join and the exclusion, with a mutation control (z2) that removes the exclusion and requires the count to become 3.

**What it does NOT do.** It names lanes from the next run onward and repairs nothing historical — the 976 untyped ids stay untyped, for the reason measured above. An untyped **main-thread** row is unaffected: it is not spawned by a `Task` call and has no `subagent_type` to carry. Counts: 14 agents / 22 commands / 41 skills / **27 hooks**.

**v15.55.0 — the duplicate guard was check-then-act, and hooks run concurrently:** the adjacent-duplicate guard in `emit-token-ledger.sh` shipped with a field measurement behind it and was never re-measured after it shipped. It read the log's last line, then appended, as two separate steps — and Claude Code runs the hooks matched to one event **concurrently**, so two firings both read before either wrote and both appended. **Measured on a real `/automate` log written by a build that HAD the guard: 13 subagent completions, 26 `token_ledger` lines, 13 adjacent byte-identical pairs, 0 of 13 suppressed.** An idle session on the same build caught most of them. That spread is a race, not a wrong rule, and it means every lane count on The Floor has been inflated up to 3x for every untyped completion — 13 stops rendering as "39 events".

**The read and the append are now one critical section**, held by a `mkdir` lock beside the log — `mkdir` because `flock` is not on stock macOS, the same reason the ui registry lock already gives. **Every failure direction is toward the append**, which is the only correct direction for an always-exit-0 advisory emitter: a lock not taken within the bounded wait (20 x 50ms) is abandoned and the append proceeds unguarded, and a lock older than a minute is broken rather than waited on. Losing a ledger line, or blocking a hook, are both worse outcomes than writing a duplicate one. The staleness check is `-mmin +1` in **whole minutes** and not a fraction: BSD find rejects `+0.16` where GNU find accepts it, which is exactly how a guard stops firing on one platform with nobody noticing — caught by running it rather than by reading it.

**Why the suite could not see this, and what closes it.** Case 19 fires the guard **sequentially** and passes against a check-then-act guard, so the defect was invisible for a release. New case 21 fires two invocations **concurrently**, with the interleaving window **injected rather than raced for** (a `sleep` spliced between the read and the append) so the case is deterministic instead of flaky. The same splice goes into both arms and the control neuters **only** the lock, so the difference in line count is attributable to the lock and nothing else. Two details are load-bearing and both were found by running it: the mutant is compared against **its own input** rather than against the shipped script — comparing against the script would pass with the lock fully intact — and the log is **seeded** first, because the guard body sits inside `if [ -f "$LOG_FILE" ]` and on a log that does not exist yet the first firing skips the window entirely and both arms return 1, a green control proving nothing. Suite: `test-token-ledger.sh` 170 -> 174. Counts unchanged: 14 agents / 22 commands / 41 skills / 26 hooks.

**Two review findings from PR #196, and the second corrects the first's stated mechanism.** The lock's cleanup trap is **chained onto** the always-exit-0 trap (`…; exit 0`), never substituted for it: `trap` replaces a handler rather than composing with it, so a bare cleanup trap discards the `trap 'exit 0' EXIT` at the top of the file — the one mechanism that makes the invariant true. The reviewer named a **signal** as the failure mode and that is not it: bash re-raises after running the EXIT trap, so an interrupted run exits 143 with the original trap, with a cleanup-only trap and with no trap at all — three measurements, identical. The real path is the ordinary one the header names: `set -u` on, `set -e` off, so an unbound variable below is a fatal non-zero. Measured there: original trap **0**, cleanup-only **1**, chained **0**. The `|| true` inside the cleanup is not a substitute — it makes the trap body succeed and the shell still exits with the fatal status. `hooks.json` appends `|| true` to all three registrations, so this is invisible in production today; a contract that holds only because of an external wrapper is the fail-safe inversion CLAUDE.md forbids. Case 22 asserts it with a control that drops only the chain. The lock is also scoped **per log file** rather than per logs directory — the duplication it serialises is two firings of one completion, which target the same file, so a directory-wide lock would serialise unrelated sessions for no correctness gain; every consumer globs `logs/*.jsonl` on the exact extension and the lock ends `.lock`. Suite: `test-token-ledger.sh` 170 -> 174.

**What this does NOT fix, stated because the obvious reading is wrong.** `identity unknown` is untouched. It was tempting to blame a missed lookup — Claude Code writes an `agent-<id>.meta.json` sidecar carrying `agentType` next to each subagent transcript, and the emitter already holds that path. Measured against a real project's whole history: 187 sidecars, 64 log `agent_id`s carrying a type, 976 carrying none; **sidecars intersect the typed set 64/64 and the untyped set 0/976.** An agent has a sidecar exactly when it already has an `agent_type` in the log, so the lookup would re-derive what is known and resolve none of what is not. Retroactive repair of existing unknown lanes is likewise impossible — no surface on disk records what those agents were.

**v15.54.0 — a token printed once is a token you cannot get back:** the page's four write buttons are gated on a per-run token, minted by `serve`, printed on one line, and held nowhere else. Everything about that was deliberate — a URL fragment is never transmitted, and the page strips it from the address bar so it cannot reach history, a bookmark or a screenshot. What none of it accounted for is that **the strip is also what puts the token-less URL into history**, so every ordinary way of coming back — a reload, a bookmark, an omnibox completion, a restored session — arrived without the token, silently demoted the page to read-only, and produced a refusal that blamed the reader for opening “a bare address” they had never typed. Worse, pasting the printed URL into the tab **already showing the page** changed only the fragment: a same-document navigation reloads nothing, the script never re-ran, and the paste was simply inert.

**Three changes, none of which gives up a property the fragment design was buying.** The page keeps the token in `sessionStorage` — per-origin, per-tab, never sent anywhere, gone when the tab closes — so a reload no longer loses it; a `hashchange` listener adopts a token appended to a page already open, which is the inert-paste case; and a token the **server** rejects is discarded rather than replayed, because a stored token can now outlast the run that minted it where a fragment never could.

**And the token is recoverable, which is the half that actually closes the loop.** `serve` records its whole `open:` line in `ui-serve.token` at mode 0600 and `check` — which is what bare `/ui` runs — reprints it while the server is up, proving liveness from the process table using `stop`'s own rule rather than from a pidfile that outlives a crash; `stop` deletes it. **The file is a SIBLING of the ui directory, never a file in it**, and that is the entire security argument: the handler serves the whole ui directory (which is why `GET /serve.log` returns the serve log), so a token file placed inside would be fetchable over the loopback port by any local process and its 0600 mode would count for nothing. Outside the served root it grants exactly the access the server's own process environment already grants that same user — the same reasoning that keeps the project registry a sibling. Serving the token over HTTP instead was considered and refused: it would let any local process ask for write access that today requires reading that user's files.

**v15.53.0 — a scope heading named paths the reader could not take away:** The Floor's rules browser grouped every rule under a heading like `scoped to CLAUDE.md, loomwright/scripts/*` — the paths were named, and there was nothing to do with them. The obvious affordance is an anchor, and it is one this page **cannot honour**: no browser follows a `file:` URL from a document served over http, and every one of them refuses **silently**, so a link would be a control that does nothing and says nothing — the same defect class as a count rendered `0` for a surface nobody read. Serving the file instead would be a fifth write endpoint, which `FLOOR_UI.md` closes by decision. **So the glob is a button, and the button says what it does: it copies the path.**

**The absolute form is joined from the served index, and the note names the base it joined against.** `floor.json` carries no project root of its own, so the page takes the directory the served index records for the document on screen — `serve`'s own launch directory, or the selected project's registered path — and states it in the note. That is not decoration: `build-floor.sh` runs in the project's **git root**, so a project registered below its repo root is exactly where that join is wrong, and naming the base makes the discrepancy visible instead of hiding it. With no base recorded the rule's own text is copied and the note says so. A glob is labelled a glob rather than presented as a file.

**The chips render only when they spell their own heading back.** `ruleScopeLabel` still owns the four-state read of `applies_to` (absent / `null` / array / malformed), and a heading it did not build from a clean array of strings — a bare-string `applies_to`, most of all — keeps its text and gets no buttons. The equality is the gate, so a second renderer cannot route around the guarantee `(j34)` protects. **No new timer:** the note persists until the next click or a project switch, because a self-clearing flash is a timer wearing a hat and this file is allowed exactly one.

**The section also says how a rule gets INTO the store** — `/rules add`, or the sole writer it delegates to (`add-rule.sh … --confirm`, which writes nothing without the flag), and the fact that **omitting `--applies-to` is what records a rule repo-wide**, which is the middle heading on that very view. It is the one thing the rules browser shows that this page cannot do, and static markup rather than projected data, so it adds no surface to `floor.json`.

**Every claim above is asserted, because a claim no check backs is the defect this repo has already paid for.** New group `(j47)`–`(j50)` in `test-setup-ui.sh`: the control exists and is gated on the heading equality; the note names its base; the bundle builds **no** anchor and names **no** `file:` scheme anywhere; and the authoring line names `/rules add`, `add-rule.sh`, `--confirm`, `--applies-to` and `repo-wide`. Two mutation controls — removing the equality gate, and planting an anchor with a `file:` href — turn the respective assertions red, so neither is a check that would pass on the page it exists to prevent. Suite: `test-setup-ui.sh` 427 → 432. Counts unchanged: 14 agents / 22 commands / 41 skills / 26 hooks.


**v15.52.0 — a lane with no role is not a lane with no identity:** every lane in The Floor's newest session read `identity unknown`, and the standing explanation was that the hook layer cannot tell which agent fired. That explanation is true of `agent_type` and was being applied to a question it does not answer. Three untyped lanes were traced to ground: each reported a `token_proxy_transcript_bytes` equal, to the byte, to the size of the **session's own transcript**; that session's directory has no `subagents/` in it at all, and its transcript contains **zero `Task` spawns**. They were never agents. The page was rendering its own question back as a missing answer.

**`agent_scope`, an additive field on both emitters, answers WHICH THREAD — a different question from `agent_type`'s WHICH ROLE.** It is not inferred from the absent type; it is read off a path the payload already carries. Claude Code writes a spawned subagent's transcript to `<session>/subagents/agent-<agent_id>.jsonl` and the session's own to `<cc_session_id>.jsonl`, so the basename is a **positive identification**: `"main"` when the payload names the session transcript, `"subagent"` when it names `agent-<agent_id>.jsonl` for that same agent, and the key **omitted** for anything else. The block is byte-parallel in `emit-token-ledger.sh` and `emit-progress-event.sh`, and it inherits the emitters' existing discipline verbatim — absent evidence is a missing key, never `""`, never `null`, never a default. `build-floor.sh` carries it into `sessions.detail.current.agents[]` the same additive way it carries `agent_type` and `branch` (taken from ANY line of that agent), with **no `schema_version` bump**: optional and additive.

**The page now says `main thread` where it used to say `identity unknown`, and only there.** Typed lanes are unchanged. An untyped lane with `agent_scope: "main"` gets the name, a plain chip instead of the dashed unknown treatment, and a `title` that states the evidence. Everything else — a spawned `subagent` whose payload carried no type, and any row nothing scoped — keeps `identity unknown` exactly as before, because those genuinely are unknown. The main-thread lane is also the one case that leaves the roster's `read-only` tri-state: the roster describes agents this plugin ships, the session thread is not one, so there is no roster row here to be missing and `read-only unknown` would answer a question that does not apply.

**The fall-through is the failure mode, so it has its own arm in both suites.** An `agent_transcript_path` that names a DIFFERENT agent must record **nothing** rather than falling through to `main` on the strength of the `transcript_path` beside it — that would mislabel a spawned agent as the session thread, the same fabrication in the opposite direction. `test-token-ledger.sh` case 20d and `test-progress-state.sh` case 40d are exactly that mutation (`elif` → `if`), and both turn red under it. The two emitters carry the block verbatim, and case 40e asserts that byte-parallelism by comparing the extracted blocks rather than trusting the prose that claims it — a one-character edit to either copy reddens it. Two further mutations were run and recorded — never recording the field reddens 20a/20b, and deleting the projector's additive clause reddens the two floor assertions. The floor fixture now carries all **three** scope states in one file (typed+subagent, untyped+main, unscoped) with a premise assertion that fails if any of the three ever disappears, and the main scope sits on an OLDER line than that agent's newest so take-from-any-line is actually exercised. On the page side, `(b30)` asserts the name is a three-way choice and `(b31)` is its mutation control. Suites: `test-token-ledger.sh` 155 → 170, `test-progress-state.sh` 189 → 204, `test-build-floor.sh` 358 → 362, `test-setup-ui.sh` 423 → 427.

**Review round (PR #191).** Two findings, both real, both from the CI reviewer. `agent_id` gates the **`subagent`** arm only — that arm claims a transcript belongs to THIS agent and cannot be answered without an id, while `main` compares against `cc_session_id` and needs none — so a session-named transcript with no `agent_id` still records `main`. Two spots in `TELEMETRY.md` had listed a missing `agent_id` as a blanket omission condition, which **overstated the guarantee**, and no case covered the combination: docs corrected to the actual rule (with why, and with the reason it misleads nobody — `build-floor.sh` builds lane rows only from lines carrying an `agent_id`), and cases **20f / 40f** added, each red under the tightening mutation. `FLOOR_UI_VERIFICATION.md`s new section had also been spliced between two bullets of AC12, orphaning its `Roster` bullet and re-pointing its closing sentence; moved below that list intact.

**Retroactive nothing, and said so in the troubleshooting table.** Lines written before this field existed carry no scope and keep reading `identity unknown`; nothing back-fills them. What closes is forward-only. Counts unchanged: 14 agents / 22 commands / 41 skills / 26 hooks.

**v15.51.0 — the owner is recorded when the run is created, not when it first logs:** v15.49.1 stopped an unrelated session from marking a LIVE run `failed`, and named the residual honestly: for **slug-keyed** runs — every `/autonomous` and `/automate` run — a run stranding between Context-Keeper's `initialize` and its first worker `SubagentStop` stayed `running` **permanently**. The cause was one missing fact. A run's owner became knowable only from the FIRST line of `.supervisor/logs/<run_id>.jsonl`, which is not written until that first worker completes, and `.supervisor/state.md` is **repo-global, not session-scoped** — so across ACQUIRE → PRE-FLIGHT SYNC → PLAN (minutes, not an instant) nothing could tell **stranded** from **live**. Both guesses were wrong and the release history has now shipped each: guessing *stranded* let any ending session stamp `failed` onto someone else's live run; guessing *live* left the stranded ones non-terminal forever. **No gate placed at `SessionEnd` can do better, because the fact it needs does not exist yet** — so this release creates it.

**`seed-run-owner.sh`, a new `PostToolUse[Write|Edit]` hook (25 → 26), writes `.supervisor/logs/<run_id>.owner`** — `cc_session_id`, `session_id`, `started_at` — the moment `.supervisor/state.md` is created. **The seed is script-owned, and that is the whole point rather than an implementation detail.** The obvious fix (have `initialize` also write the session id) puts a fact a HOOK must rely on into an AGENT PROMPT, which this repo has measured: 560 hook-written `token_ledger` events vs 6 agent-written `phase_transition` events. An agent also cannot know its own Claude Code session id — the hook payload is the only place it appears.

**The trigger was measured against a live `claude -p` session before a line was written, because the whole design rests on two harness properties.** Context-Keeper has no Bash (`disallowedTools`), so the **Write tool is the only way** `initialize` can create the file, and it runs as a SUBAGENT. Both were verified: `PostToolUse[Write]` **does** fire for a subagent's Write (the observed payload carried `agent_type: general-purpose` and an `agent_id`), and that payload's `session_id` is **byte-identical** to the `SessionEnd` payload's for the same CLI session — which is exactly the value the close-out compares against later. That makes the trigger **causally exact rather than a race**: the session that wrote the run's state file is its owner by definition. A seed hung off the already-firing `PostToolUse[Bash]` matcher was considered and **REJECTED** for precisely the opposite reason — any session running any Bash command would have been an equally good claimant, which is the false attribution this exists to close, re-opened in a new place.

**It is a sidecar, not a log line, and write-once.** The log may ALREADY have a first line by the time `state.md` exists (`/autonomous` appends `autonomous_session_start` at INIT), the log is append-only so line 1 cannot be reclaimed, and a new JSONL event type would grow a case in `build-insights.sh`, `build-loop-evidence.sh`, `build-floor.sh` and every other consumer — all of which glob `*.jsonl` explicitly, so `.owner` is inert to them, in a directory that already holds `telemetry.log`, `worktrees.log` and the telemetry `.flag` markers. Creation is atomic (`set -C` → `O_EXCL`), so a run's owner cannot change under it and Context-Keeper's many later edits are no-ops. It is never seeded for a terminal run, and **never for a run whose log already records an owner** — a `/supervisor --continue` resume must not contradict the original.

**Where the seed and the log's first line disagree, the seed wins — and the disagreement IS the bug.** While an owner is unknown the emitters ADOPT (deliberately: the fresh-run bootstrap), so a FOREIGN session's event can land as the log's first line and make that session look like the owner. Only the two STRICT consumers apply this precedence — the `SessionEnd` close-out and `build-state.sh` — because they are the ones a wrong owner can make write a false verdict.

**Both consumers named in the residual now work in that window, with no rule loosened.** The close-out takes its ordinary known-owner path; the unknown-owner self-identification demand from the previous release is untouched and still guards every unseeded run. The staleness backstop, which used to return on `build-state.sh`'s log-exists guard before deriving anything, now reaches the window because the seed supplies an owner AND a `started_at` that counts as owner-originated evidence — the last thing we know the owner did when it has emitted no events of its own; the LATER of {newest owner line, `started_at`} is measured, so a log that does carry owner lines stays authoritative. **That branch is deliberately narrow: with no log it may write ONLY `failed`/`LOOP`** — the same pair a `session_end` produces — **never `running`**, so it cannot stamp the terminal phase word onto a live run mid-PLAN, and it never brings a `state.md` into existence. `reproject-state-on-terminal.sh` gains the matching cheap arm (seed mtime instead of log tail when there is no log), because nothing else can invoke the projector there and a KILLED terminal never reaches `SessionEnd` either. **No new `status` or `phase` word anywhere; `paused` is still never emitted.**

**Controls, because the property that matters is a NEGATIVE one.** In-suite, 5a proves a slug-keyed run with no log now closes out for its own session — structurally impossible before — while **5b proves an unrelated session still writes nothing**, 5c isolates the seed as the authorising fact (remove it and 5a's session writes nothing), and 5d/5e pin the precedence in both directions. On the projector side 38a/38b are the same pair (stranded closes, live stays byte-identical, PHASE included). Four code mutations were run and recorded: restoring **unconditional adopt-on-unknown** turns **13** assertions red (11 pre-existing plus 5c's pair); making a **known owner stop excluding a foreign ending session** turns **10** red including 5b reporting the live run as `failed` — the defect reproduced verbatim; deleting the **pre-event write gate** turns 38b red with `PLAN` overwritten by `LOOP`; deleting its **run-id equality check** turns 38d red. One control is honestly downgraded rather than left overclaiming: deleting the reproject hook's mtime guard entirely leaves the suite **189/189 green**, because `build-state.sh` refuses the same case independently — so that guard is a COST gate, not a correctness one, and the comment now says so. Suites: `test-progress-state.sh` 161 → 189, `test-close-stranded-run.sh` 67 → 86, plus a new `test-seed-run-owner.sh` at 45 — which also asserts the two copies of `loom_run_owner_seed` are byte-identical, since both files claim they are kept so.

**What remains is stated in `TELEMETRY.md` §"Honest limits" entry 9 rather than implied closed.** A run with **no seed** — anything whose `state.md` predates this release — keeps the old behaviour verbatim; the seed is forward-only and nothing retro-fits an owner. A killed terminal's run is now **bounded** (up to `LOOMWRIGHT_STALE_RUN_SECONDS`, plus the next Bash call in that checkout) rather than permanent. And **the two emitters are deliberately NOT changed**: they still read the log's first line alone and still ADOPT on unknown, which means their gate is **inert for `/autonomous` runs**, whose first log line carries no `cc_session_id`. Neither the close-out nor the projector is fooled by the foreign appends that permits, but the log still grows — closing it is the same three-line fallback in `loom_log_owner`, it would STRENGTHEN rather than weaken adopt-on-unknown, and it is named as its own change because those two emitters carry an explicit non-negotiable and the load-bearing suites. Counts: 14 agents / 22 commands / 41 skills / **26 hooks**.

**v15.50.0 — The Floor people can actually read, and a `serve` the agent can actually run:** two defects of the same shape — the thing works, and is unusable as delivered.

**`/ui serve` could not work through the slash command at all.** `do_serve` without `--detach` ends in `wait "$srv"` under a `trap … EXIT INT TERM`. That is correct for a human in their own terminal, but `/ui` is a slash command whose **only caller is an agent**: the call blocks to the tool timeout, the tool kills the process, and the `EXIT` trap then **kills the server it just started** — while "Ctrl-C to stop" is advice the user has no way to follow, because it is not their shell. The engine was correct and is unchanged; the defect was that the command bodies restated the engine's CLI semantics without saying **which caller** they describe. Five prose surfaces are now caller-scoped and the two *invocation* surfaces carry `--detach`. The fifth surface, `commands/agent-help.md`, was found only on the second review pass — the recorded agent↔command mirror-drift class — and a new gate now scans `commands/*.md` **and** `scripts/*.sh`, with a control proving the engine's own runtime `foreground: Ctrl-C to stop` is seen and **not** flagged, so the command-body-vs-runtime distinction is a real discrimination rather than a clause satisfied for free by a file glob.

**The page is restyled into the loomwright-site design language.** Warm cream surfaces, the site's hairline and radius vocabulary, and its uppercase letter-spaced mono labels, verified from the live DOM rather than asserted: `body` computes to `rgb(244,235,220)` = the site's `--ink`. **The site's three typefaces could not cross** — it loads them from Google Fonts and ships no local files, while this bundle forbids `@font-face`, remote refs and a fourth file — so the Floor uses the fallback stacks the site itself declares (Impact / Georgia / `ui-monospace`), which is exactly what the site renders as with no network. The site is light-only; **both themes are kept**, with the dark palette derived into the same warm family rather than left cold grey.

**Contrast was measured, and three pairs failed and were fixed by moving the style, not the bar:** the lane shuttle (2.71:1 → 5.25:1), the selected-project rule (2.20:1 → 5.59:1), and every form control's edge, which was a decorative `--hair` at ~1.30:1 — acceptable for a card boundary, not for the edge that identifies a control. Two pairs remain below threshold and are **recorded rather than omitted**: `--hair` as pure decoration, which 1.4.11 does not reach, and the active-stage rule, which is the *fourth* redundant channel on that state after the phase word, its weight and the card's shape.

**An accessibility property that no test backed now has one.** The skip link, the banner's `role=status`/`aria-live=polite` and the section label associations were asserted **nowhere** — `skip-link` had zero hits in the suite, `aria-live` exactly one as a comment *excluding* it, and `(j36)`'s label loop read its section list **from** `aria-labelledby`, so a section losing the attribute dropped out of the input and the check passed vacuously. The new guard is **per-section**, not two document-wide counts: a global count can be refilled from a landmark while a section goes bare. Its comment-stripper is multi-line-capable, because all four comments in `index.html` span lines and the reflexive one-line `sed` strips nothing — measured 7 before, 7 after.

Counts unchanged at 14 agents / 22 commands / 41 skills: `/ui` gained no verb.

**v15.49.1 — the close-out could mark a LIVE run failed, because `state.md` is repo-global:** v15.49.0 stopped a finished run from capturing later sessions' events. This closes the same false-attribution hole pointed the other way, found by review on that PR. `close-stranded-run.sh` treated an unknown log owner as adoptable — the direction the two emitters take, and correct **for them**. But `.supervisor/state.md` is **repo-global, not session-scoped**: every session anchored to the same main worktree reads the same `- session_id:` / `- status: running`. Before a run's first worker completion creates `logs/<id>.jsonl` there is no owner to compare against, so session A could start a run and an entirely unrelated session B, **merely by ENDING**, would satisfy every remaining condition and stamp `status: failed` onto A's live run — leaving a fabricated hard-signal `session_end` (which `build-insights.sh` consumes) that nothing retracts. Two terminals on one checkout is an ordinary way to use this plugin, so this was reachable, not theoretical.

**An unknown owner is now adoptable only by a session whose payload `session_id` IS the run id `state.md` names.** The narrower rule review first proposed — demand that equality unconditionally — was **measured and rejected**: `state.md` session ids exist in TWO shapes on disk, a Claude Code uuid AND a plugin slug (`auto-2026-09-05-050440`, `20260426-004614-supervisor`), and a uuid payload can never equal a slug, so an unconditional demand would have silently disabled close-out for every slug-keyed autonomous run. Applying it **only on the unknown-owner path** leaves the known-owner main path — what any real run reaches after its first event — completely untouched. **The emitters are deliberately NOT changed:** they still ADOPT on unknown owner, because their protected property is the fresh-run bootstrap (non-negotiable 2) and the worst they can do is mis-file an event. The close-out is the one writer that can mark a live run dead, so it alone demands self-identification. Three previously-adopting degenerate cases follow from the same rule and now withhold: an empty/unparseable payload against an unknown owner, a **broken `jq`** (zero identity information — and a jq-less host has already lost ownership, the emitters' gate, and the staleness backstop, so inventing a `failed` would be the one unrecoverable move left to it), and a **malformed first log line** paired with an unrelated session.

**Residual, stated precisely because the loose reading understates it** (`TELEMETRY.md` §"Honest limits" entry 9): for **slug-keyed** runs — every `/autonomous` and `/automate` run, whose `state.md` id is a synthetic `auto-2026-09-05-050440` minted by the loop and seeded at `initialize` — a `SessionEnd` payload always carries the real Claude Code uuid, so it can **structurally never** match, and the unknown-owner path is unreachable rather than occasionally missed. The exposed window is `initialize` through the first worker `SubagentStop` (ACQUIRE → PRE-FLIGHT SYNC → PLAN: minutes, not an instant), and a run stranding there stays `running` permanently — the staleness backstop cannot rescue it either, for a second reason beyond having no owner lines: the projector returns early on its log-exists guard, and in this window the log does not exist at all. **This is not a regression, it is the same missing fact from the other side:** with no owner recorded, nothing can tell "stranded" from "live" — the old behaviour guessed *stranded* (the false-close-out defect above), this one guesses *live*, and no gate placed at `SessionEnd` can do better. The real fix is to record the owning session id **at run creation**, which is tracked as follow-up rather than bolted on here, because the seed lives in an agent prompt and `docs/PITFALLS.md` records why agent-written state is the wrong mechanism for a fact a hook must rely on. This guess is preferred only because its failure is the recoverable one: a stale `running` is an advisory label a human can reset, whereas a live run marked `failed` writes a false hard signal `build-insights.sh` consumes and nothing retracts. Suite `test-close-stranded-run.sh` 55 → 67 with 4m/4n/4o added; **4m is the control** that stops the rest from passing under a guard that merely disabled the path. Mutation control recorded: restoring unconditional adopt turns **11** assertions red, `case4n` among them reporting the live run's status as `failed` — the defect reproduced verbatim. Counts unchanged at 14 agents / 22 commands / 41 skills / 25 hooks.

**v15.49.0 — A run that ends stops owning the log:** `.supervisor/state.md` sat at `status: running` / `phase: EXECUTE` for a run started **2026-07-29** whose brief was already in `jobs/done/` and which never emitted a `session_end`. That single stale line was not cosmetic, because both emitters joined a run's log whenever the status read `running`/`checkpoint` — so every later session's `SubagentStop` appended into that one file, measured at **14,416 lines, 3.9 MB, 140 distinct `cc_session_id`s**. `build-state.sh` then derived status from the newest of `{subtask_complete, session_end}` in that same log, which was always a fresh *foreign* `subtask_complete`, and re-asserted `running`. **The stale status caused the fan-in and the fan-in re-asserted the stale status** — a closed loop that no amount of resetting `state.md` by hand would have broken, and which made The Floor render ordinary interactive chat turns as a live Supervisor run.

**The gate is ownership, and the owner is written on the log's first line.** `emit-token-ledger.sh` and `emit-progress-event.sh` now resolve the `cc_session_id` of the first line of `.supervisor/logs/<plugin session>.jsonl`; only that session may join it, and a mismatch blanks the plugin session id so the firing lands in its own `<cc_uuid>.jsonl` while the owned log's line count does not move. **Unknown owner ADOPTS, and that direction is load-bearing rather than lenient** — an absent, empty or unreadable log, an unparseable first line, a first line with no `cc_session_id`, or a `jq` that is missing or cannot execute (probed functionally, never by `command -v` alone) all mean "adopt", because refusing would regress the very first worker completion of every fresh run, when the log does not exist yet and there is nothing to be the owner of. The gate is placed deliberately *below* the status `case` block in both emitters: that `running|checkpoint)` line is a CI-pinned citation target in root `CLAUDE.md` and in `emit-progress-event.sh`'s own header, and inserting above it would have silently drifted both pins.

**Stopping the fan-in does not close a stranded run, so the other half is mechanical too.** The plugin registers its **first `SessionEnd` hook** (24 → 25), running the new `close-stranded-run.sh`: when `state.md` is non-terminal and this session owns the run's log, it appends exactly one `session_end` carrying **both** the canonical `event` and the legacy `type` key (the contract `build-insights.sh` filters on), `status: failed`, `reason: session_ended_without_completion`, then re-projects. A terminal status, an absent state.md, or a non-owner session writes nothing. `paused` is deliberately never emitted — it is classified live by `hook-dispatch-on-pr-create.sh` and dead by both emitters, so emitting it would put two consumers in disagreement about one file. And because a killed terminal never reaches `SessionEnd` at all, `build-state.sh` gains `LOOMWRIGHT_STALE_RUN_SECONDS` (default 86400): a derived `running` whose newest **owner-originated** event is older than the threshold becomes `failed`. **Owner-only measurement is the whole point** — measuring over all lines is precisely what made the original loop circular, since foreign appends kept a finished run's log looking fresh forever. No new status word is introduced anywhere.

**A close-out that fires on `/clear` would corrupt state in the opposite direction, so it is scoped in two places.** `SessionEnd` fires for `clear` as well as for real termination, and `cc_session_id` is stable across `/clear` within one CLI process — so a user clearing context mid-run presents a non-terminal `state.md` with a *matching* owner, satisfying every condition above, and an unscoped hook would stamp `status: failed` onto a run that is still executing. `hooks.json` registers the hook under `"matcher": "logout|prompt_input_exit|other"`, and `close-stranded-run.sh` independently exits 0 writing nothing when the payload's `.reason` is exactly `clear`. That second layer is a **deny-list and not an allow-list**, which is the load-bearing detail: requiring the reason to be a known-terminal value would make an *absent* `reason` skip, and the no-`reason` payload is the main path, not an edge case. Absent, empty, or unparseable reason therefore proceeds — a stranded run is recoverable via the staleness backstop, a wrongly-closed live run is not. The `clear` case and a `logout` control are both pinned, the control specifically so the guard cannot pass as a blanket suppression of every reason-bearing payload.

**One gap is documented rather than closed.** A close-out whose `SessionEnd` payload arrives empty or unparseable cannot prove ownership, so a log with a *known* owner — the normal state of any run past its first worker completion, i.e. exactly the incident above — takes the non-owner branch and stays stranded. Writing anyway was rejected for the same reason as the `clear` case: the script cannot tell "my payload was lost" from "another, still-live session owns this", and the second reading is unrecoverable. The staleness backstop is the named recovery path, and the behaviour is pinned by a test so a future change to the ownership branch has to confront it instead of altering it silently.

Two smaller things ride along, both **measured rather than assumed**. Ledger lines were mostly unattributed because the real `SubagentStop` payload usually carries no `agent_type`, and **both** emitters now resolve it the same way — from the **payload only**, with **no env fallback in either** — never inventing it. A `LOOMWRIGHT_AGENT_TYPE` mechanism (a hook entry injecting its own matcher name into `emit-progress-event.sh`) was tried and **REMOVED**: measurement showed the matcher does not discriminate. Grouping untyped events by `agent_id` on the live log gives a fixed **2 `token_ledger` : 1 `subtask_complete`** in every bucket, so the single `loomwright:worker` block runs on the same untyped payloads as the three ledger blocks — registration under one matcher prevents duplication, but proves nothing about discrimination, and injecting the name would have stamped `worker` onto thousands of non-worker completions. `emit-token-ledger.sh` never had the fallback for the same reason: its three matchers discriminate *only* when the payload already carries an `agent_type`, so adopting the identity of whichever matcher happened to run would be a guess in exactly the population that has no identity — and it would defeat the byte-identity dedupe, since two lines differing only in a fabricated `agent_type` can never compare equal. In both emitters “omitted” means absent entirely, never empty-string and never null. The accepted consequence, documented rather than papered over: **most Floor lanes still read `identity unknown`**, because the hook layer genuinely cannot tell which agent fired. The long-suspected duplicate ledger line is explained far enough to fix it: those three matchers are **not** mutually exclusive, and on the live log **94/94** typed firings emitted exactly one line while **4,376/4,376** untyped firings emitted exactly two — so more than one block runs for an untyped payload. Why exactly two and not three is recorded as an **open question** in `TELEMETRY.md` rather than guessed. The fix matches the mechanism — skip an append that is byte-identical to the log's current last line, consecutive-only because the duplicate blocks fire back-to-back, and O(1) because it reads one line. Suites: `test-progress-state.sh` 117 → 161, `test-token-ledger.sh` 111 → 155, plus a new `test-close-stranded-run.sh` at 55; the ownership and `clear`-scoping assertions are mutation-controlled — reverting either guard alone turns them red. The known limit is documented rather than hidden in `TELEMETRY.md` §"Run ownership": **a run resumed under a different Claude Code session id will no longer join its original log.** Counts: 14 agents / 22 commands / 41 skills / **25 hooks**.

**v15.48.0 — The registry lock: two writers, and both edits kept:** v15.47.0 gave the page a write path, which made a deferred assumption reachable. Every registry edit is a read-modify-write — snapshot the JSON, compute the new document, write a temp file, `mv` it into place — and the `mv` makes each write **atomic**: no reader ever sees a half-written registry, and a crash mid-write leaves the previous document intact. **Atomic is not serialised.** Two invocations racing on one registry each snapshot the *same* starting document, and whichever `mv` landed last won, silently discarding the other's `add` or `forget` rather than erroring or merging. A terminal running `/ui add` and the page running the same verb through the server are two processes, and v15.47.0's in-flight flag is per-page — it closes a double-click inside one document and provably not two tabs on one token URL. The previous release stated the assumption in `FLOOR_UI.md` and deferred the fix; this release closes it, and that paragraph is now a pointer to the mechanism rather than a caveat.

**The lock is `mkdir`, because `flock` is not on stock macOS** — and `mkdir` is atomic on every POSIX filesystem, so exactly one of N racing callers creates `<registry>.lock` and the rest see `EEXIST`. **It is taken before the snapshot, not around the write:** a lock covering only the `mv` would serialise two writers that had already read the same document, which is the same lost update with extra steps. The **read** verbs (`check`, `list`, an unconfirmed `scan`) take nothing — a reader is already safe here because the registry only ever becomes visible whole, via `mv`, and locking reads would buy contention plus a wedged writer blinding every view of the registry.

A lock brings two failure modes this engine could not have before, and **both are handled by name and both still exit 0**, because the fail-safe contract is refuse-and-say-why, never a non-zero exit. **A stale lock** — left by a process killed between `mkdir` and its release — is broken, and the *three* ways to be stale get three sentences rather than one: the recorded owner **has exited**; the recorded pid is alive but **belongs to a different program** (reused after the holder died); or the lock **records no owner and is older** than the few seconds a healthy holder can take between creating it and writing its pid. A lock whose owner is alive and *is* this engine is busy, not stale — and so is one the engine cannot judge because `ps` said nothing: **refusing to judge means waiting**, which is the only direction that cannot destroy a registry. Breaking is done by *renaming the lock aside*, one `rename(2)`, so two processes that both decide the same lock is stale cannot both proceed. **A deadlock or a slow holder** is bounded by the new `--lock-timeout <n>` (default `10`, `0` meaning do not wait) and ends in a refusal that names the lock path and the timeout and has written nothing. **Release is structural, not per-path:** one `EXIT`/`INT`/`TERM` trap, so a `return` branch added later cannot forget it — which is what lets the refusal branches (including the one taken with the lock already held, when the registry turns out to be unparseable) be asserted rather than audited.

**The new gate group `(o)` is a measurement, not an inspection,** because a concurrency control cannot be shown with one invocation. Two `add`s really do run concurrently, and **the race is made deterministic by the environment rather than by editing the engine**: a `jq` on PATH that sleeps before exec'ing the real one widens the critical section to seconds while **the engine under test stays byte-identical**, so the subject of the primary assertion is the shipped file. Both registrations must survive *and* the pair must take materially longer than a single writer measured in the same run — serialisation asserted, not inferred from a lucky outcome. **The control changes exactly one token** so the write path takes no lock, under the same slow `jq` with the same two projects, and one registration is then lost; it additionally requires *both* mutant writers to have reported success, since "one entry" is also what one dead writer looks like. Staleness gets **four** cases, not three: the three breaking verdicts, plus the arm proving an ownerless-and-*fresh* lock is left alone — without which a detector that called every lock stale would pass the other three while destroying the mechanism. Suite 341 → 363 — then 373, after a follow-up fixed two defects in the suite itself: (k28) false-failed whenever the developer's own Floor was running, and the teardown reaped by pidfile, which a refused `stop` deletes. Counts unchanged at 14 agents / 22 commands / 41 skills / 24 hooks: this adds a flag, not a verb.

**v15.47.0 — The floor acts: guarded writes from the page:** Item 06 made the page *show* the project registry; it still could not touch it. The blocker was structural, not cosmetic — the module served the bundle with `python3 -m http.server`, a static file server that answers `501` to every write, so there was no endpoint to call. Item 04 had also justified having no authentication with "there is nothing to authenticate against on loopback". **That was sound for a read-only page and stops being sound the moment a write exists**, which is the real content of this release: a loopback port is not a security boundary in a browser. Any site open in another tab can `fetch` a POST at `127.0.0.1:<port>` — the attacker never reads the reply, but the write has already landed — and DNS rebinding extends the same reach to a remote origin. So this was not "add some buttons"; it was earning the right to have them.

The static server is replaced by a stdlib `ThreadingHTTPServer` + `SimpleHTTPRequestHandler` subclass (no new dependency, no Node, no npm, no CDN — item 04's constraint intact). Every `GET` is preserved byte-for-byte and unrouted writes still return `501`. **Exactly four mutating routes exist — `add`, `forget`, `scan`, `stop` — and the set is read out of the engine rather than restated in the test**; `apply` and `remove` are excluded *by decision, not omission*, because a page able to uninstall itself buys nothing and risks something.

**The guard is four parts and all four are required together**, because any one alone is defeatable: a per-run bearer token (minted by `serve`, never written world-readable, never logged, never in the served HTML), **sent in a custom request header** so a CORS preflight a hostile cross-origin page cannot satisfy is forced — closing the "simple request" hole where a write lands even though the attacker never reads the reply — plus **`Origin`** validation and **`Host`** validation, the latter being what actually defeats DNS rebinding, which an `Origin` check provably cannot. **Each part is proven load-bearing by a control that disables that part alone and watches the previously-refused request succeed** (`(n23)`–`(n26)`, each returning 200 against the mutant where the shipped engine returns 403), with `(n27)` failing red if any of those four controls is ever silently skipped. The token travels in the URL `#fragment` — never transmitted to any server, so it cannot reach a request line or a log — and the page strips it via `history.replaceState`.

Paths supplied through the browser are treated as untrusted input: resolved, confined to the serving process's home directory, and refused by name for traversal, non-directories, and symlinks escaping the root — while **the command line stays deliberately unconfined**, because the terminal is the trusted channel and the browser is not. That asymmetry is documented, not incidental.

**Eight existing gate groups had their subject replaced by this change, and every one was repaired rather than deleted or relaxed** — the enumeration was the hard part, and it took three rounds of plan review to get right: a list assembled from memory found two, a sweep for *the tokens a scanner matches* found four (structurally blind to a re-run of that scanner 1,400 lines away), and only a sweep for **scanner names and assertion subjects** found all eight. Two of them fail *silently* — `(h2)` and `(l8)` build their mutants by exact string match into the file being changed, so once that string is gone `mutant_ok` returns false and the control is skipped rather than reported red — so the repairs assert that `mutant_ok` still returns true. `(j14)` held a private inlined copy of the `no_write_verbs` regex and would have gone quietly vacuous the moment the shared scanner was re-baselined; it now calls the shared scanner, as does `(l19)`, so the two cannot drift. `(h1)` was re-expressed against the new bind expression keeping its exactly-one shape, and `(a4)`'s fetch-call-site count was re-baselined to an exact `2` with its controls deriving from that constant. **R1's uncovered path is now covered:** `do_stop` only kills a process whose command line matches the module's, and replacing the server changed that command line — a break no test would have caught, since coverage of that refusal path went from **0 to 7**.

`serve` now writes one further file, `<ui dir>/serve.log` — one line per *mutating* request plus one line for a request that could not be answered at all (`FloorServer.handle_error` overrides `socketserver`'s traceback-printing default, so neither a pre-authentication `TypeError` nor a client that hung up mid-reply can put a multi-line, thread-interleaved traceback into a file that sits inside the served root), never a caller-supplied string, completed reads not logged — added because the criterion "the token never appears in the server's own output" was unfalsifiable while that output went to `/dev/null`. Both exhaustive write lists name it, and a new gate reads the name out of the engine so the list stays mechanically exhaustive. Counts unchanged at 14 agents / 22 commands / 41 skills / 24 hooks: `/ui` gained no verb — the page gained the ability to invoke ones that already existed.

**v15.46.0 — `/ui`: the floor in one command, and one floor for every project:** The Floor (v15.44.0) was reachable only the long way. `serve` and `stop` — daily *operations* — lived behind `/setup ui`, a *configuration* command, and in practice that pushed people back to `bash loomwright/scripts/setup-ui.sh serve` in a terminal, which is the exact friction the module existed to remove. Meanwhile the bundle was global but the data was per-directory: `apply` copies the bundle to one user-scope directory and `serve` regenerated `floor.json` from whatever directory it was launched in into that one shared slot, so two projects collided on it and only the port guard (which refuses to move the port rather than serve wrong bytes) kept that from being silent.

**The new `/ui` command** (commands 21 → 22) owns the operational half — `check` / `serve` / `stop` — plus a **project registry**: `add` / `list` / `forget` / `scan`. `apply` and `remove` stay with `/setup ui`, because they are the two verbs that materialise and delete a directory of files outside the repo, and both remain marker-gated behind an explicit confirm. **The split is asserted, not merely described.** A new `test-setup-ui.sh` group `(m) AC-entrypoint-parity` parses the engine's real subcommand set out of its own dispatch `case` and requires (i) every verb documented in `commands/setup.md`'s `## Module: ui` flow to exist in it, (ii) every verb documented in `commands/ui.md` to exist in it, and (iii) the **union** of the two documented sets to **equal** it — so a verb the engine gains and neither file documents FAILS. Two details are load-bearing: the parse is **pinned** to `case "$SUBCMD" in` (the engine has eight `case` statements and reading the wrong one silently yields a nonsense verb set), and it reads the **arm labels**, never the `*)` arm's error string, which is a second in-file enumeration that can drift on its own — with a control proving a drifted error string does not move the parsed set.

**The obvious mutation control provably does not work, and that is recorded rather than glossed.** Deleting one of `check`/`apply`/`serve`/`stop`/`remove` from `commands/ui.md` does **not** redden, because `commands/setup.md` already documents all five and the gate tests the union. The two controls that do work are therefore: **(c1)** delete a verb documented only in `commands/ui.md` (one of the registry verbs) and require the union clause to redden; **(c2)** append an undocumented verb to a scratch copy of the engine's dispatch and require the same clause to redden — the engine-side drift, which otherwise has no control at all.

**One server on one port now shows any registered project**, chosen in the page rather than by which directory a terminal was launched in. A project appears **only because a human ran `add`** (or confirmed a `scan`); there is no discovery-on-serve. The registry `projects.json` lives *beside* the ui directory rather than inside it, so `/setup ui` → `remove` cannot take the project list with it — asserted directly rather than reasoned from the path. `forget` is deliberately **not** named `remove`: one word meaning both "tear down the module" and "drop a project from a list" is a data-loss shape, and the project tree is hashed before and after to prove `forget` touches nothing on disk. The selected project regenerates on `--interval` and the others one at a time on a slower cadence, because one projector run costs about a second and regenerating every project every tick starves a 2 s loop at **two** projects — measured across a fixed tick count against a stub projector of known cost, with a control proving the naive shape really is over budget and a null-mutation control proving a harmless change stays within it. Per project, `ready` / `never regenerated` / `unavailable` / `unreadable` stay four distinct renders, and the registry's own *absent* and *unparseable* stay two different sentences. Selecting a project **sends nothing** — it changes which already-written `projects/<slug>/floor.json` the page fetches — and the bundle still carries no `POST`, no `PUT`, no `DELETE` and no `fetch` with a `method` option, on loopback only.

**Release surface.** The `(z)` parity group is EXTENDED at `(z10)`–`(z15)` rather than replaced: the `/ui` registry row in `commands/agent-help.md`, the command's own flow document, the module docs' cross-reference to the second entry point, a residue sweep for the superseded `21 commands` claim, its anti-vacuity companion asserting the current claim is actually made, and — closing AC-3d — a grep asserting that `docs/FLOOR_UI.md`'s `## What it writes — the whole list` names the served index `index.json` that v15.44.0's successor introduced, so that section stays the exhaustive list it claims to be with a mechanical subject rather than a promise. `docs/FLOOR_UI.md` gains a `## Projects` section. Counts: 14 agent roles, **22 slash commands**, 41 skills, 24 quality gate hooks. **Measured ratchet delta** (`bash scripts/check-vendor-coupling.sh --print-allowances`, run after `git add` of every new file, never hand-typed): **unchanged** — `loomwright/scripts/setup-ui.sh` stays 3 and `loomwright/docs/FLOOR_UI.md` stays 4, both matching the committed manifest, and `loomwright/scripts/test-setup-ui.sh` still holds no entry at all. That is the SECOND measurement: the first showed the suite file moving 0 → 1 because a comment quoted the host-tool root variable literally, which would have put the first coupling entry on a file the manifest records as holding none — the comment was reworded to describe the path forms rather than quote one, and no allowance was raised to accommodate prose. `loomwright/commands/` is adapter-classified, so the new command file moves nothing. **Suites:** `test-setup-ui.sh` 201 → 290 passed, 0 failed, 0 skipped; `test-build-floor.sh` unchanged at 348 passed, 0 failed, 0 skipped. All 72 `test-*.sh` exit 0 and all seven `scripts/check-*.sh` gates exit 0. Counts: 14 agents / 22 commands / 41 skills / 24 hooks.

**v15.45.0 — Archive views: the accumulated judgement, made browsable, never a scoreboard:** The Floor (v15.44.0) answered "what is happening now"; the larger asset was what the system had already *learned*, and it was readable only by opening files — `.agent/rules/` is machine-readable and human-hostile, and `.supervisor/postmortem/results.jsonl` is the single best record of *why* this system produces churn, with nothing displaying it. Three pieces ship, strictly additive over the existing `floor.json` contract (`schema_version` stays 1). **The projector** extends the `rules` and `postmortem` surfaces with an optional `detail` via the existing `add_surface` seam: `rules` gains tri-state `applies_to`/`check` fields (decided by key **presence**, never truthiness — absent, present-with-`null`, and a non-empty value are three distinguishable renders) and a supersession walk that DELIBERATELY DIVERGES from `read-rules.sh`'s routing semantics — it hides nothing, resolves transitively and in order, and REPORTS a dangling pointer instead of ignoring it, because a curation-history browser needs the opposite answer from a routing reader. It inherits that reader's one non-negotiable property regardless: every walk is the same bounded, non-recursive fixed-length pass per node, so a malformed or cyclic `supersedes` graph can never hang the real store. `postmortem` gains a class/flow-stage distribution plus the per-category `evidence` string, with the flow-stage basis PINNED to `.categories[].flow_stage` and published as a literal `flow_stage_basis` field — the ledger carries a second, disagreeing representation (measured: 26 of 89 lines, every one an engine-drain line), and publishing the wrong one silently would be the fabricated-single-truth defect this changelog has recorded before. An unparseable rules file is NAMED with its own reason, never silently folded into "clean" or dropped from the count. **A 4.1x regression (0.85s → 3.5s) was caught only by hand** after the detail passes shipped: redundant re-scans of the same two stores, fixed by consolidating onto shared passes (back to ~1.1s), with every emitted number verified byte-identical to an independent out-of-band measurement. The new runtime bound that prevents a repeat is a **ratio against a real artefact** (the pre-fix commit itself, not a hand-built mutant that might represent nothing) — an absolute wall-clock ceiling was rejected first because it is unusable in both directions (CI hardware differs from a laptop), and timing the small fixtures was rejected because jq startup dominates and would go green straight through the very regression it exists to catch. **The bundle** adds a rules browser (grouped by category then by `applies_to` scope, showing the supersession history including cycles and dangling pointers) and a churn view (class/flow-stage distributions sorted by key, never by count) reading only the projection fields above — `flow_stage_basis` is read off the projection rather than restated as a literal. Every correlation shown is a labelled observation carrying its evidence; nothing is ranked, scored, or sorted by desirability, and an uncomputable correlation is omitted rather than emitted as a zero that would read as "measured no violations". All four absent/empty/unavailable/stale renders get their own fixture and their own literal text, and a new read-only assertion (measured 0 `POST`/`PUT`/`DELETE` occurrences before this change, so the check could not have inherited a pass) closes the one AC-still-read-only had left uncreated. No new timer, fetch, or endpoint — the single-`setInterval` budget and the egress scanner both still pass unchanged. **`/handoff`** gains an opt-in `--publish` flag that emits the existing catch-up digest a second time as a shareable, point-in-time snapshot (`.supervisor/handoff/snapshot.md`) — the same decision/why/tried-rejected/current-state/provenance content, wrapped in a banner naming its own generation time and explicitly disclaiming polling. **Byte-identity of the default path is proven mechanically, not by inspection:** the pre-flag script is pinned at a real commit and diffed against the post-change default output on identical fixture inputs, with a mutation control proving the diff itself can discriminate, and the one field that legitimately varies run-to-run (the wall-clock generation timestamp, which already varied before this change) is named and excluded rather than silently smoothed over. Absent the flag, no new code path executes. `commands/handoff.md` documents the flag in the same commit that adds it, closing a mirror-drift class no gate here catches mechanically. **Measured ratchet deltas** (`bash scripts/check-vendor-coupling.sh --print-allowances`, run after `git add` of everything, never hand-typed): only `loomwright/scripts/test-build-handoff.sh` moved, 11 → 14 (+3, CORE) — the three new occurrences are the harness variable naming the plugin install root, passed into the pre-change and post-change script invocations exactly as the pre-existing test helper already does. Every other path in scope for this release is unchanged: `loomwright/scripts/build-handoff.sh` stays 5 (the publish flag adds no new reference to that variable, only a guarded write under the existing output directory), `loomwright/scripts/setup-ui.sh` stays 3, `loomwright/docs/FLOOR_UI.md` stays 4, and `loomwright/docs/RESULT_SCHEMAS.md` stays 3. `loomwright/scripts/test-setup-ui.sh` (~370 new lines this release) and `loomwright/scripts/floor-ui/*` were explicitly re-measured rather than assumed unchanged, and both remain at zero — the bundle and its self-test name no host-tool path at all, exactly as the prior release's own note already established. **Suites:** `test-build-floor.sh` 265 → 348 passed, 0 failed, 0 skipped **on a full checkout** — 329 passed, 0 failed, 1 skipped in a fresh worktree or on CI, where `.supervisor/` is gitignored and the local-only real-tree corroboration case skips with a named reason. Both figures are stated because the environment is what separates them, and the earlier one alone would be a number CI never reproduces; `test-setup-ui.sh` 119 → 201 passed, 0 failed, 0 skipped; `test-build-handoff.sh` 41 → 65 passed, 0 failed, 0 skipped. Every `loomwright/scripts/test-*.sh` exits 0 and all seven `scripts/check-*.sh` gates exit 0. Counts unchanged: 14 agents / 21 commands / 41 skills / 24 hooks.

**v15.44.0 — The Floor: a run you can watch, and a page that never claims more than it recorded:** the plugin's run state became legible in v15.43.0 (`build-floor.sh` → `.supervisor/floor/floor.json`) but nothing rendered it, so a stall was still discovered only when a human noticed nothing had happened for a while. **`/setup ui`** is the tenth `/setup` module: it copies a three-file, framework-free bundle (`index.html` + `floor.css` + `floor.js` — no bundler, no Node, no package manager, no web font) into the ui directory and serves it with `python3 -m http.server --bind 127.0.0.1`, regenerating `floor.json` on a 2 s interval. Five pipeline stages (Queue · Plan · Execute · Review · Shipped) sit above one lane per agent in the newest session.

**MOTION IS EVIDENCE, NEVER DECORATION, and that is the design's spine.** There is exactly ONE timer on the page — the poll — and a lane's shuttle advances only when that lane's recorded `events` count changed between two renders. Verified in a real browser with a control: bumping one lane's count advanced only that lane and left the unchanged lanes still. The single stated exemption is the pulse on a non-stalled lane, whose ABSENCE is the stall signal, so it is state-driven even though a keyframe drives its frames; the static suite asserts that budget by OCCURRENCE COUNT (one `setInterval`, zero `requestAnimationFrame`) with a mutation control adding a second timer.

**THE PAGE NEVER CLAIMS MORE THAN THE RECORD SUPPORTS.** There is no liveness field and no "currently running agent": no spawn event exists in any log, so liveness could only be inferred from some other record's recency — a guess — and the page renders that sentence permanently under the lanes. `agent_type` is carried by a small minority of events, so an untyped lane is drawn `identity unknown` with a dashed chip rather than assigned a plausible role; browser-verified, the typed lanes rendered in their agents' own frontmatter colours (`worker` #32CD32 solid, `code-reviewer` #20B2AA hollow **because it is read-only**) beside a neutral third lane labelled "identity unknown". `read_only` on a roster row is a genuine TRI-STATE — `true`, `false`, or **omitted entirely** when the agent declares no `disallowedTools` line — and the third renders "read-only unknown", never silently as "not read-only". A lane's number is EVENTS, not turns and not progress.

**Absent, empty, unavailable and stale are four distinct renders, never a blank page:** `no floor.json at this origin` (404), `no run in flight` (earned only when the `sessions` surface is **counted** AND carries a `detail.current` view with zero agents in it) and `session data unavailable — <the projector's own reason>` (every other no-lane shape: the surface absent or `unverified`, or `current` omitted — the page states what it could not see rather than asserting idleness) and `floor.json is stale (<age>)`. The first three were browser-verified against committed fixtures with the console read for errors. **`?stale=<seconds>` exists for a measured reason** and is documented alongside `?stall=<seconds>`: a committed fixture's `generated_at_epoch` is always in the past, so without it every fixture renders under the stale banner and nothing else about the page can be demonstrated.

**Loopback is the whole security posture and it is not negotiable at the command line:** `serve` always passes `--bind 127.0.0.1`, there is no flag to change it and no code path that omits it; `index.html` carries a `default-src 'self'` CSP and `floor.js` fetches only the relative path `floor.json`. The suite scans all three bundle files for absolute URLs, protocol-relative references inside `src`/`href`/`url(`, `@import`, preconnect and `@font-face`, with a mutation control proving the scanner can fail — and a real browser network log of a page load showed requests to 127.0.0.1 only. This matters concretely because `floor.json` carries **branch names, session ids and agent ids**; the local-only posture is stated in the new `docs/FLOOR_UI.md`.

**The engine is marker-gated, and the marker exists because `--ui-dir` is user-supplied.** `apply` writes `.loomwright-ui-module`; `apply` and `remove` both WITHHOLD on a directory carrying no marker, and `remove` additionally refuses `/`, `$HOME` and anything at or under the plugin install directory — a typo pointing at a real directory must not cost the user that directory. Every branch exits 0 (`serve: ABORTED — python3 not found`, a busy port REPORTED and never silently moved, `build-floor.sh` missing named by reason), which is the `setup-statusline.sh` fail-safe convention: refuse-to-write plus a named reason, never a non-zero exit. Residue is proven by hashing TWO whole trees with `find` before and after a full `apply` → `serve` → `stop` → `remove` sequence — the fixture parent and a fixture git repo used as the working directory — asserting the only difference is the one regenerated `.supervisor/floor/floor.json`, plus an anti-vacuity check that the write actually happened.

**Projector extension (additive, `schema_version` stays 1):** a new `agents` surface reads agent frontmatter into a roster (`name`, `color`, `model`, `max_turns`, `read_only`), `sessions.detail.current` aggregates per-`agent_id` rows for the newest session, and `state.detail.subtasks` emits `{id, title, status}` in table order — every field OMITTED when absent, never defaulted. `add_surface` no longer returns silently on invalid `detail` JSON: the surface is still emitted without it and a note names the key and the reason. The suite stays hermetic by construction: `FLOOR_AGENTS_DIR` is exported into every fixture run so the EMPTY fixture still yields an `absent` agents surface and the anti-vacuity control stays green — a real-dir default would have counted the real agents on the empty fixture and turned that control red.

**Measured ratchet delta** (`bash scripts/check-vendor-coupling.sh --print-allowances` run after staging every new file, never hand-typed): `loomwright/scripts/setup-ui.sh` = 3 (new, CORE), `loomwright/docs/FLOOR_UI.md` = 4 (new, COUPLED), `loomwright/skills/setup/SKILL.md` 38 → 40 (+2, COUPLED). The bundle and the self-test hold NO entry, and that is measured rather than assumed — the page names no host-tool path at all and every test case passes an explicit `--ui-dir` into a `mktemp -d`, which is also what proves the suite can never reach a developer's real config tree. **The module-registration surfaces now have a gate:** `check-command-sync.sh` guards only one file and `check-doc-currency.sh` derives no module count, so a `(z)` release-surface parity group was appended to `test-setup-ui.sh` asserting the `ui` registry row, the `## Module: ui` flow, the bundled-status recipe and zero `9 modules` residue across the three files that carried it. Suites: `test-build-floor.sh` 258 assertions, `test-setup-ui.sh` 74 + the parity group. Counts unchanged: 14 agents / 21 commands / 41 skills / 24 hooks; `/setup` modules 9 → 10.

**v15.43.0 — nine surfaces, nine parsers, and a number that meant two different things:** the run state a view needs is already real and already on disk, but it is scattered across **nine surfaces in four formats** — markdown tables (`.supervisor/state.md`), folder membership (the four `jobs/` lifecycle dirs), JSONL (`logs/*.jsonl`, `postmortem/results.jsonl`) and JSON (`drain-rounds/`, `.agent/rules/`), plus `automate/`, `insights/runs/` and `worker-summaries/`. With no single reader, every future view re-implements the same nine parsers and drifts independently. **`build-floor.sh`** is that reader and `.supervisor/floor/floor.json` is the contract; it mirrors `build-insights.sh` / `build-handoff.sh` exactly — `set -uo pipefail` WITHOUT `set -e`, a `command -v jq` guard that skips rather than fails, read-only on every input, **exit 0 always**. Three properties carry it. **(1) Every count states its own counting basis, because a bare number was already ambiguous.** `.supervisor/logs/` holds **99 directory entries** but **65 `*.jsonl`** session logs — the other 34 are plain `.log` dispatch transcripts. Both readings are defensible, they differ by 34, and the source requirement picked the other one; so `logs: 65` now ships with the glob that produced it and can never be confused with `logs: 99`. **(2) Absent evidence is an omitted key, never a plausible default.** A surface that cannot be counted carries **no `count` key at all** — never `0` — and names the reason; an input that will not parse reports `unverified`, never `counted`. The `mtime_epoch()` helper copies `build-handoff.sh`'s GNU-`stat -c %Y`-then-BSD-`stat -f %m` **order** (load-bearing: on GNU `-f` means *filesystem status* and succeeds printing a mount point, the bug that once shipped macOS-green and Linux-CI-red) but deliberately **not its call sites**, which do `${e:-0}` — defaulting an unverifiable mtime to the Unix epoch is precisely the lie this projector exists to refuse. A `stat` stub printing `/` must make the field OMITTED: not empty, not `0`, not the raw string. **(3) Sessions segment by the `cc_session_id` FIELD, never by filename** — a log file is named for a `state.md` `status:` that can read `running` for weeks, so one file routinely spans many sessions across many branches. No emitter change was needed; the field is already additive on every event. **A real defect was caught by the design rather than by luck:** `jq -e .` takes its exit status from the last OUTPUT value, so a perfectly valid document whose value is `null`, `false` or `0` exits 1 and would have been reported as malformed.
